Before this project, my routines never interfered with my life or got in the way of things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these intense worries and checking behaviors started after you began the museum commission; do you think that if the project were to be completed or put on hold, your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors would significantly decrease or disappear?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't really thought about whether pausing or completing the project would make all of this go away. Right now, the anxiety and these checking loops are just so overwhelming that I can't see past them. I'm just too stuck in the middle of it to know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were to make a mistake in your project, the consequences would be so severe that they would be catastrophic or unbearable, or is it more about the fear of not meeting your own high standards of perfection?\n\nPatient: It is honestly a mix of both. On one hand, I feel a deep fear that making a mistake would mean disrespecting the elders or misrepresenting Black Southern history, which feels completely unbearable to me. It's just that these checking loops and the exhaustion take up so much of my time and energy that I simply can't keep up with anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period in your life where you didn't have these obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or have they been present in some form throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I've always had a perfectionistic streak that actually helped me with my work, but it was completely manageable. These intense, uncontrollable worries and checking behaviors only really took over my life in the last several months since I started this high-profile museum commission. Before this project, my routines never interfered with my life or got in the way of things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these intense worries and checking behaviors started after you began the museum commission; do you think that if the project were to be completed or put on hold, your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors would significantly decrease or disappear?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't really thought about whether pausing or completing the project would make all of this go away. Right now, the anxiety and these checking loops are just so overwhelming that I can't see past them. I'm just too stuck in the middle of it to know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were to make a mistake in your project, the consequences would be so severe that they would be catastrophic or unbearable, or is it more about the fear of not meeting your own high standards of perfection?\n\nPatient: It is honestly a mix of both. On one hand, I feel a deep fear that making a mistake would mean disrespecting the elders or misrepresenting Black Southern history, which feels completely unbearable to me. At the same time, my own perfectionism drives me to keep checking and adjusting things over and over until they feel \"just right.\"\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your symptoms are primarily triggered by your museum project, but can you think of any other situations or themes outside of this project that might trigger similar obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors, or is it exclusively related to the project's details and perfection?\n\nPatient: While I’ve always had a perfectionistic streak in my past work, like when I did grant writing or organized installations, it never felt like this. These distressing, loop-like thoughts and checking behaviors are currently entirely focused on this high-profile museum project. It's specifically the fear of making a mistake in these historical details that triggers everything.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific historical details or aspects of the museum project that you find yourself obsessing over more than others, or is it a broad range of details that trigger your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors?"

It's just that these checking loops and the exhaustion take up so much of my time and energy that I simply can't keep up with anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period in your life where you didn't have these obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or have they been present in some form throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I've always had a perfectionistic streak that actually helped me with my work, but it was completely manageable. These intense, uncontrollable worries and checking behaviors only really took over my life in the last several months since I started this high-profile museum commission. Before this project, my routines never interfered with my life or got in the way of things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these intense worries and checking behaviors started after you began the museum commission; do you think that if the project were to be completed or put on hold, your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors would significantly decrease or disappear?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't really thought about whether pausing or completing the project would make all of this go away. Right now, the anxiety and these checking loops are just so overwhelming that I can't see past them. I'm just too stuck in the middle of it to know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were to make a mistake in your project, the consequences would be so severe that they would be catastrophic or unbearable, or is it more about the fear of not meeting your own high standards of perfection?\n\nPatient: It is honestly a mix of both. On one hand, I feel a deep fear that making a mistake would mean disrespecting the elders or misrepresenting Black Southern history, which feels completely unbearable to me. At the same time, my own perfectionism drives me to keep checking and adjusting things over and over until they feel \"just right.\"\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your symptoms are primarily triggered by your museum project, but can you think of any other situations or themes outside of this project that might trigger similar obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors, or is it exclusively related to the project's details and perfection?\n\nPatient: While I’ve always had a perfectionistic streak in my past work, like when I did grant writing or organized installations, it never felt like this. These distressing, loop-like thoughts and checking behaviors are currently entirely focused on this high-profile museum project. It's specifically the fear of making a mistake in these historical details that triggers everything.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific historical details or aspects of the museum project that you find yourself obsessing over more than others, or is it a broad range of details that trigger your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: It is very specific to making sure I accurately represent Black Southern history. I constantly obsess over whether I misquoted an elder, got a historical date wrong, or somehow disrespected the community's story. This leads me to repeatedly check the text in my grant proposal and re-measure the physical placement of the exhibit labels and artifacts.\n\nDoctor: If you were to take on a new project that didn't involve historical details or representation of a specific community, do you think you would experience similar obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or would they be significantly reduced?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, as I haven't even thought about what it would be like to do a different kind of project. Right now, my mind is so overwhelmed by the pressure of this current commission that I can't even picture how I would react to anything else."

What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: I keep going over the same worries about getting some detail wrong in my project, and it’s crowding out everything else in my day. I'll even whisper the facts under my breath like a checklist until it feels \"just right,\" and I can't seem to stop even when I'm completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that these repetitive actions or rituals are aimed at preventing some specific, dreaded outcome, or are they more about trying to achieve a sense of perfection or control over your project?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. On one hand, I’m terrified of making a factual error, misquoting an elder, or disrespecting the history of the community I’m representing. But on the other hand, I have to repeat the physical re-reading and re-measuring until it feels \"just right\" to ease my anxiety and feel in control.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you experience excessive anxiety or fear in situations outside of your project, such as social interactions or everyday decisions, or if it's primarily focused on the details and perfection of your work?\n\nPatient: The anxiety and checking are almost entirely focused on getting the details of my museum project exactly right. While I have been avoiding social events like church and my guild, it's not because of social anxiety or fear of those interactions. It's just that these checking loops and the exhaustion take up so much of my time and energy that I simply can't keep up with anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period in your life where you didn't have these obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or have they been present in some form throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I've always had a perfectionistic streak that actually helped me with my work, but it was completely manageable. These intense, uncontrollable worries and checking behaviors only really took over my life in the last several months since I started this high-profile museum commission. Before this project, my routines never interfered with my life or got in the way of things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these intense worries and checking behaviors started after you began the museum commission; do you think that if the project were to be completed or put on hold, your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors would significantly decrease or disappear?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't really thought about whether pausing or completing the project would make all of this go away. Right now, the anxiety and these checking loops are just so overwhelming that I can't see past them. I'm just too stuck in the middle of it to know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were to make a mistake in your project, the consequences would be so severe that they would be catastrophic or unbearable, or is it more about the fear of not meeting your own high standards of perfection?\n\nPatient: It is honestly a mix of both. On one hand, I feel a deep fear that making a mistake would mean disrespecting the elders or misrepresenting Black Southern history, which feels completely unbearable to me. At the same time, my own perfectionism drives me to keep checking and adjusting things over and over until they feel \"just right.\"\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your symptoms are primarily triggered by your museum project, but can you think of any other situations or themes outside of this project that might trigger similar obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors, or is it exclusively related to the project's details and perfection?\n\nPatient: While I’ve always had a perfectionistic streak in my past work, like when I did grant writing or organized installations, it never felt like this. These distressing, loop-like thoughts and checking behaviors are currently entirely focused on this high-profile museum project. It's specifically the fear of making a mistake in these historical details that triggers everything.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific historical details or aspects of the museum project that you find yourself obsessing over more than others, or is it a broad range of details that trigger your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: It is very specific to making sure I accurately represent Black Southern history. I constantly obsess over whether I misquoted an elder, got a historical date wrong, or somehow disrespected the community's story. This leads me to repeatedly check the text in my grant proposal and re-measure the physical placement of the exhibit labels and artifacts.\n\nDoctor: If you were to take on a new project that didn't involve historical details or representation of a specific community, do you think you would experience similar obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or would they be significantly reduced?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, as I haven't even thought about what it would be like to do a different kind of project. Right now, my mind is so overwhelmed by the pressure of this current commission that I can't even picture how I would react to anything else.\n\nDoctor: If you were to imagine yourself completing this museum project to your satisfaction and then moving on to a completely different type of project that doesn't involve historical details or community representation, do you think you would still experience these obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or would they significantly decrease?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. Part of me hopes that it would get better because the pressure of this specific project is so intense, but another part of me worries that my perfectionism might just find something else to fixate on."

Before this project, my routines never interfered with my life or got in the way of things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these intense worries and checking behaviors started after you began the museum commission; do you think that if the project were to be completed or put on hold, your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors would significantly decrease or disappear?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't really thought about whether pausing or completing the project would make all of this go away. Right now, the anxiety and these checking loops are just so overwhelming that I can't see past them. I'm just too stuck in the middle of it to know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were to make a mistake in your project, the consequences would be so severe that they would be catastrophic or unbearable, or is it more about the fear of not meeting your own high standards of perfection?\n\nPatient: It is honestly a mix of both. On one hand, I feel a deep fear that making a mistake would mean disrespecting the elders or misrepresenting Black Southern history, which feels completely unbearable to me. At the same time, my own perfectionism drives me to keep checking and adjusting things over and over until they feel \"just right.\"\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your symptoms are primarily triggered by your museum project, but can you think of any other situations or themes outside of this project that might trigger similar obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors, or is it exclusively related to the project's details and perfection?\n\nPatient: While I’ve always had a perfectionistic streak in my past work, like when I did grant writing or organized installations, it never felt like this. These distressing, loop-like thoughts and checking behaviors are currently entirely focused on this high-profile museum project. It's specifically the fear of making a mistake in these historical details that triggers everything.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific historical details or aspects of the museum project that you find yourself obsessing over more than others, or is it a broad range of details that trigger your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: It is very specific to making sure I accurately represent Black Southern history. I constantly obsess over whether I misquoted an elder, got a historical date wrong, or somehow disrespected the community's story. This leads me to repeatedly check the text in my grant proposal and re-measure the physical placement of the exhibit labels and artifacts.\n\nDoctor: If you were to take on a new project that didn't involve historical details or representation of a specific community, do you think you would experience similar obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or would they be significantly reduced?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, as I haven't even thought about what it would be like to do a different kind of project. Right now, my mind is so overwhelmed by the pressure of this current commission that I can't even picture how I would react to anything else.\n\nDoctor: If you were to imagine yourself completing this museum project to your satisfaction and then moving on to a completely different type of project that doesn't involve historical details or community representation, do you think you would still experience these obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or would they significantly decrease?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. Part of me hopes that it would get better because the pressure of this specific project is so intense, but another part of me worries that my perfectionism might just find something else to fixate on.\n\nDoctor: If you were to imagine that someone else took over the responsibility for the historical accuracy and representation in your museum project, would your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors significantly decrease or remain the same?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure because I haven't even thought about that as an option. This responsibility feels so intensely personal and tied to my own standards that it's hard to even imagine handing it over to someone else. Because of that, I don't know if my worries would actually go away or not."
  }
]

It's specifically the fear of making a mistake in these historical details that triggers everything.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific historical details or aspects of the museum project that you find yourself obsessing over more than others, or is it a broad range of details that trigger your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: It is very specific to making sure I accurately represent Black Southern history. I constantly obsess over whether I misquoted an elder, got a historical date wrong, or somehow disrespected the community's story. This leads me to repeatedly check the text in my grant proposal and re-measure the physical placement of the exhibit labels and artifacts.\n\nDoctor: If you were to take on a new project that didn't involve historical details or representation of a specific community, do you think you would experience similar obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or would they be significantly reduced?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, as I haven't even thought about what it would be like to do a different kind of project. Right now, my mind is so overwhelmed by the pressure of this current commission that I can't even picture how I would react to anything else.\n\nDoctor: If you were to imagine yourself completing this museum project to your satisfaction and then moving on to a completely different type of project that doesn't involve historical details or community representation, do you think you would still experience these obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or would they significantly decrease?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. Part of me hopes that it would get better because the pressure of this specific project is so intense, but another part of me worries that my perfectionism might just find something else to fixate on.\n\nDoctor: If you were to imagine that someone else took over the responsibility for the historical accuracy and representation in your museum project, would your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors significantly decrease or remain the same?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure because I haven't even thought about that as an option. This responsibility feels so intensely personal and tied to my own standards that it's hard to even imagine handing it over to someone else. Because of that, I don't know if my worries would actually go away or not.\n\nDoctor: If someone else were to review and confirm the accuracy of the historical details in your project, would you be able to accept their confirmation and stop your repetitive checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't thought about that. Even if someone else confirmed it was correct, I doubt it would stop the urge to check because everything still has to feel \"just right\" to me before I can let it go."
  }
]

At the same time, my own perfectionism drives me to keep checking and adjusting things over and over until they feel \"just right.\"\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your symptoms are primarily triggered by your museum project, but can you think of any other situations or themes outside of this project that might trigger similar obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors, or is it exclusively related to the project's details and perfection?\n\nPatient: While I’ve always had a perfectionistic streak in my past work, like when I did grant writing or organized installations, it never felt like this. These distressing, loop-like thoughts and checking behaviors are currently entirely focused on this high-profile museum project. It's specifically the fear of making a mistake in these historical details that triggers everything.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific historical details or aspects of the museum project that you find yourself obsessing over more than others, or is it a broad range of details that trigger your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: It is very specific to making sure I accurately represent Black Southern history. I constantly obsess over whether I misquoted an elder, got a historical date wrong, or somehow disrespected the community's story. This leads me to repeatedly check the text in my grant proposal and re-measure the physical placement of the exhibit labels and artifacts.\n\nDoctor: If you were to take on a new project that didn't involve historical details or representation of a specific community, do you think you would experience similar obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or would they be significantly reduced?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, as I haven't even thought about what it would be like to do a different kind of project. Right now, my mind is so overwhelmed by the pressure of this current commission that I can't even picture how I would react to anything else.\n\nDoctor: If you were to imagine yourself completing this museum project to your satisfaction and then moving on to a completely different type of project that doesn't involve historical details or community representation, do you think you would still experience these obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or would they significantly decrease?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. Part of me hopes that it would get better because the pressure of this specific project is so intense, but another part of me worries that my perfectionism might just find something else to fixate on.\n\nDoctor: If you were to imagine that someone else took over the responsibility for the historical accuracy and representation in your museum project, would your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors significantly decrease or remain the same?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure because I haven't even thought about that as an option. This responsibility feels so intensely personal and tied to my own standards that it's hard to even imagine handing it over to someone else. Because of that, I don't know if my worries would actually go away or not.\n\nDoctor: If someone else were to review and confirm the accuracy of the historical details in your project, would you be able to accept their confirmation and stop your repetitive checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't thought about that. Even if someone else confirmed it was correct, I doubt it would stop the urge to check because everything still has to feel \"just right\" to me before I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: If an expert in Black Southern history were to review your project and assure you that everything is accurate and respectful, would you feel able to stop your repetitive checking behaviors and consider the project complete?\n\nPatient: An expert's reassurance might help for a brief moment, but it probably wouldn't stop my checking for long because the doubt always creeps back in. Even though I logically know I've already checked the details a dozen times, my brain still asks \"what if\" I made a mistake. I would still feel compelled to keep re-reading and re-measuring until it feels \"just right.\""
  }

But on the other hand, I have to repeat the physical re-reading and re-measuring until it feels \"just right\" to ease my anxiety and feel in control.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you experience excessive anxiety or fear in situations outside of your project, such as social interactions or everyday decisions, or if it's primarily focused on the details and perfection of your work?\n\nPatient: The anxiety and checking are almost entirely focused on getting the details of my museum project exactly right. While I have been avoiding social events like church and my guild, it's not because of social anxiety or fear of those interactions. It's just that these checking loops and the exhaustion take up so much of my time and energy that I simply can't keep up with anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period in your life where you didn't have these obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or have they been present in some form throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I've always had a perfectionistic streak that actually helped me with my work, but it was completely manageable. These intense, uncontrollable worries and checking behaviors only really took over my life in the last several months since I started this high-profile museum commission. Before this project, my routines never interfered with my life or got in the way of things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these intense worries and checking behaviors started after you began the museum commission; do you think that if the project were to be completed or put on hold, your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors would significantly decrease or disappear?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't really thought about whether pausing or completing the project would make all of this go away. Right now, the anxiety and these checking loops are just so overwhelming that I can't see past them. I'm just too stuck in the middle of it to know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were to make a mistake in your project, the consequences would be so severe that they would be catastrophic or unbearable, or is it more about the fear of not meeting your own high standards of perfection?\n\nPatient: It is honestly a mix of both. On one hand, I feel a deep fear that making a mistake would mean disrespecting the elders or misrepresenting Black Southern history, which feels completely unbearable to me. At the same time, my own perfectionism drives me to keep checking and adjusting things over and over until they feel \"just right.\"\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your symptoms are primarily triggered by your museum project, but can you think of any other situations or themes outside of this project that might trigger similar obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors, or is it exclusively related to the project's details and perfection?\n\nPatient: While I’ve always had a perfectionistic streak in my past work, like when I did grant writing or organized installations, it never felt like this. These distressing, loop-like thoughts and checking behaviors are currently entirely focused on this high-profile museum project. It's specifically the fear of making a mistake in these historical details that triggers everything.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific historical details or aspects of the museum project that you find yourself obsessing over more than others, or is it a broad range of details that trigger your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: It is very specific to making sure I accurately represent Black Southern history. I constantly obsess over whether I misquoted an elder, got a historical date wrong, or somehow disrespected the community's story. This leads me to repeatedly check the text in my grant proposal and re-measure the physical placement of the exhibit labels and artifacts.\n\nDoctor: If you were to take on a new project that didn't involve historical details or representation of a specific community, do you think you would experience similar obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or would they be significantly reduced?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, as I haven't even thought about what it would be like to do a different kind of project. Right now, my mind is so overwhelmed by the pressure of this current commission that I can't even picture how I would react to anything else.\n\nDoctor: If you were to imagine yourself completing this museum project to your satisfaction and then moving on to a completely different type of project that doesn't involve historical details or community representation, do you think you would still experience these obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors, or would they significantly decrease?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. Part of me hopes that it would get better because the pressure of this specific project is so intense, but another part of me worries that my perfectionism might just find something else to fixate on.\n\nDoctor: If you were to imagine that someone else took over the responsibility for the historical accuracy and representation in your museum project, would your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors significantly decrease or remain the same?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure because I haven't even thought about that as an option. This responsibility feels so intensely personal and tied to my own standards that it's hard to even imagine handing it over to someone else. Because of that, I don't know if my worries would actually go away or not.\n\nDoctor: If someone else were to review and confirm the accuracy of the historical details in your project, would you be able to accept their confirmation and stop your repetitive checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't thought about that. Even if someone else confirmed it was correct, I doubt it would stop the urge to check because everything still has to feel \"just right\" to me before I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: If an expert in Black Southern history were to review your project and assure you that everything is accurate and respectful, would you feel able to stop your repetitive checking behaviors and consider the project complete?\n\nPatient: An expert's reassurance might help for a brief moment, but it probably wouldn't stop my checking for long because the doubt always creeps back in. Even though I logically know I've already checked the details a dozen times, my brain still asks \"what if\" I made a mistake. I would still feel compelled to keep re-reading and re-measuring until it feels \"just right.\"\n\nDoctor: If you were completely convinced by an outside authority that your project was perfect and accurate, do you think you could stop your obsessive thoughts and compulsive checking behaviors, or would they persist regardless of the reassurance?\n\nPatient: I believe they would persist regardless of any outside reassurance. Even though I already intellectually know I've checked the details a dozen times and nothing new comes of it, the internal anxiety is just too strong. I would still feel driven to keep replaying the thoughts and re-checking everything until it feels \"just right.\""
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F42.2"], "note": "The patient's symptoms, such as repetitive checking behaviors and obsessive thoughts focused on achieving perfection in their project, are consistent with a specific condition. The fact that these symptoms are currently entirely focused on the museum project and are driven by a fear of making mistakes and a need for control, suggests that the condition is likely related to obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors. The patient's perfectionism and the specificity of their obsessive thoughts to the project's historical details also support this diagnosis. While the patient's symptoms are significant and interfere with their daily life, the fact that they are primarily triggered by the project and may decrease if the project were to be completed or put on hold, suggests that the condition may be related to a specific situation rather than a more general condition. Previously, the candidates included a code for a condition characterized by persistent and excessive worry, but the patient's symptoms and behaviors are more consistent with the specific condition characterized by obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors. Therefore, the current candidate is more likely to be the correct diagnosis.", "is_final": true}
